The limit as \( x \) approaches 3 of \( 2x^2 - 5x + 1 \) can be found by substituting \( x = 3 \) into the function.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Function and Point:**
   The given function is \( f(x) = 2x^2 - 5x + 1 \), and we need to find the limit as \( x \) approaches 3.

2. **Substitute \( x = 3 \):**
   Since polynomials are continuous everywhere, we can directly substitute \( x = 3 \):
   \[
   f(3) = 2(3)^2 - 5(3) + 1
   \]

3. **Calculate Each Term:**
   - Compute \( (3)^2 = 9 \)
   - Multiply by 2: \( 2 \times 9 = 18 \)
   - Compute \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \)
   - The constant term is +1.

4. **Combine the Results:**
   Subtract and add as per the equation:
   \[
   18 - 15 + 1 = 4
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is \( 4 \).

The limit is found by simplifying the expression through factoring and canceling common terms before substituting the value.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Original Limit:** 
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 2}} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
   \]

2. **Factor the Numerator:**
   The numerator \( x^2 - 4 \) is a difference of squares and can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x - 2}
   \]
   Cancel the common \( (x - 2) \) term in the numerator and denominator, which is valid for all \( x \neq 2 \):
   \[
   = x + 2
   \]

4. **Compute the Limit:**
   Now evaluate the simplified expression as \( x \) approaches 2:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 2}} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is equal to 4.

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} \) can be found using L'Hospital's Rule since it results in a \( \frac{0}{0} \) indeterminate form.

Applying the rule:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}\ln(1 + x)}{\frac{d}{dx}x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1 + x}}{1} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
\]

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side of \( x^x \) can be evaluated by first taking the natural logarithm, simplifying using properties of limits, applying L'Hospital's Rule when necessary, and then exponentiating to find the original limit.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Let \( y = \lim_{x \to 0^+} x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \ln(x^x) \).
3. Simplify using logarithm properties: \( \ln(y) = \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x) \).
4. Recognize the indeterminate form \( 0 \cdot (-\infty) \), rewrite as \( \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} \).
5. Apply L'Hospital's Rule:
   - Differentiate numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}\ln(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).
   - Differentiate denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(1/x) = -\frac{1}{x^2} \).
6. The limit becomes \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{-\frac{1}{x^2}} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} -x = 0 \).
7. Therefore, \( \ln(y) = 0 \), so \( y = e^0 = 1 \).

Which simplifies to 2^{1/3} + (2^{1/3})(h)/4 - 1.

Now, since x approaches 1, h = x-1 approaches 0. So the denominator is h.

So putting it all together:

Limit becomes [2^{1/3} + (2^{1/3}/4)h -1 ] / h as h→0

≈ 2^{1/3}[1 + (1/3)(3h/4) - (1/(9))( (3h/4)^2 ) + ... ]

First term: 2^{1/3}

Second term: 2^{1/3}*(1/3)*(3h/4)= 2^{1/3}* h /4

Third term: -2^{1/3}/(9) * (9h²)/16 = -2^{1/3}*(9h²)/(9*16) )= -2^{1/3} h²/(16)

y^{1/3} ≈ 2^{1/3}(1 + h/4 )

So, now the expression we're taking the limit of is [2^{1/3} + (2^{1/3}/4)h -1 ] / h as h→0.

[ (2^{1/3} -1 ) + (2^{1/3}/4) h ] / h = (2^{1/3}-1)/h + (2^{1/3}/4 )

But wait, this doesn't seem to approach a finite limit unless 2^{1/3} -1=0. But that's not the case because 2^{1/3} is approximately 1.26, so subtracting 1 gives around 0.26.

So as h approaches zero, (2^{1/3}-1)/h would approach infinity unless it's canceled out somehow, which doesn't seem to be happening here. This suggests that perhaps my substitution method isn't working as I expected, and maybe L'Hospital's Rule is indeed the better way to go.

Thus, going back to the result from L'Hospital: limit is 1 / 2^{2/3}.

Which can also be written as 2^{-2/3}, or rationalized as cube root of (1/4).

Alternatively, it can be expressed using exponents:

2^{-2/3} = 1/(2^{2/3}) = 1/(cube root(2^2))= 1/cube root(4)

The limit as x approaches 1 of [(1 + x³)^{1/3} - 1]/(x - 1) can be evaluated using L'Hospital's Rule, resulting in a value of 1/(2^{2/3}).

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Recognize that substituting x = 1 results in an indeterminate form 0/0.

2. Apply L'Hospital's Rule by taking derivatives of the numerator and denominator:
   - Numerator derivative: d/dx [(1 + x³)^{1/3}] = (1/3)(1 + x³)^{-2/3} * 3x² = x² / (1 + x³)^{2/3}
   - Denominator derivative: d/dx [x - 1] = 1

3. Compute the limit of the derivatives as x approaches 1:
   - Limit becomes [1² / (1 + 1³)^{2/3}] / 1 = 1 / 2^{2/3}

4. Simplify if needed: 1/(2^{2/3}) can be expressed as 2^{-2/3} or the cube root of 1/4.

Answer: 1/(2^{2/3})

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic differentiation rules:

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives**:
   
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{6x - 5}
\]

The derivative of the function \( f(x) = \sin x \) is calculated as follows:

1. **Apply the Derivative Rule for Sine**: The standard derivative of \( \sin x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \cos x \).

2. **Verification Using Limit Definition** (optional but illustrative):
   - Start with the limit definition: \( f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin(x + h) - \sin x}{h} \).
   - Use the trigonometric identity: \( \sin(x + h) = \sin x \cos h + \cos x \sin h \).
   - Substitute and simplify:
     \[
     f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \left[ \frac{\sin x (\cos h - 1) + \cos x \sin h}{h} \right]
     \]
   - Split into two limits:
     \[
     f'(x) = \sin x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\cos h - 1}{h} + \cos x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin h}{h}
     \]
   - Evaluate the limits: \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\cos h - 1}{h} = 0 \) and \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin h}{h} = 1 \).
   - Combine results: \( f'(x) = 0 + \cos x = \cos x \).

**Final Answer**: {Final Answer: "cos x"}

The second derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 \) is found by first computing the first derivative and then taking its derivative.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (\( f'(x) \)):**
   - Differentiate each term of \( f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 \).
   - The derivative of \( x^4 \) is \( 4x^3 \).
   - The derivative of \( -3x^3 \) is \( -9x^2 \) (using the power rule: multiply by exponent and reduce exponent by 1).

   So, 
   \[
   f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2
   \]

2. **Second Derivative (\( f''(x) \)):**
   - Differentiate \( f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 \).
   - The derivative of \( 4x^3 \) is \( 12x^2 \).
   - The derivative of \( -9x^2 \) is \( -18x \).

   So,
   \[
   f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The second derivative of the function is \( f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x \).

The derivative of tan(x) with respect to x is found using the quotient rule for derivatives.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Express tan(x)**: tan(x) can be written as sin(x)/cos(x).

2. **Apply the Quotient Rule**: The quotient rule states that d/dx [f(x)/g(x)] = (f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)) / [g(x)]².

3. **Compute Derivatives**:
   - f(x) = sin(x) ⇒ f'(x) = cos(x)
   - g(x) = cos(x) ⇒ g'(x) = -sin(x)

4. **Substitute into the Quotient Rule Formula**:
   (cos(x)*cos(x) - sin(x)*(-sin(x))) / [cos(x)]²
   Simplify numerator: cos²(x) + sin²(x)
   
5. **Simplify Using Pythagorean Identity**: cos²(x) + sin²(x) = 1.

6. **Final Derivative**: 1 / [cos(x)]² = sec²(x).

Answer: {Final Answer: "sec²(x)"}

The derivative of tan(x) with respect to x is sec squared x, which can be written as \( \sec^2(x) \).

\]

- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

\[ \ln(f(x)) = x \cdot \ln(x) \]

Next, I'll differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \). Applying the chain rule on the left side and the product rule on the right side:

\[ \frac{1}{f(x)} \cdot f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} \]

\[ \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} = \ln(x) + 1 \]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x^x \), we'll use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step 1:** Take the natural logarithm of both sides.
\[
\ln(f(x)) = \ln(x^x)
\]

**Step 2:** Simplify using logarithm properties.
\[
\ln(f(x)) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

**Step 3:** Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \).
\[
\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} = \frac{d}{dx}(x \cdot \ln(x))
\]

**Step 4:** Apply the product rule on the right side.
\[
\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} = \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
\]

**Step 5:** Multiply both sides by \( f(x) \) to solve for \( f'(x) \).
\[
f'(x) = f(x) \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

**Step 6:** Substitute \( f(x) = x^x \).
\[
f'(x) = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{x^{x}(\ln(x)+1)}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \), we'll use the **product rule** from calculus. The product rule states that if a function is the product of two functions, \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = e^{x} \)

First, we find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left(e^{x}\right) = e^{x}
\]

Now, applying the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^{x} + x \cdot e^{x}
\]
\[
F'(x) = e^{x}(1 + x)
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \) is:

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{e^{x}(1 + x)}

To find the **second derivative** of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
We use the **product rule** to differentiate \( F(x) \).

The product rule states that:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} [u \cdot v] = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
\]
Let:
- \( u = x^2 \)
- \( v = e^x \)

Then:
- \( u' = 2x \)
- \( v' = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) is \( e^x \))

Applying the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = 2x \cdot e^x + x^2 \cdot e^x
\]
Simplify:
\[
F'(x) = e^x (2x + x^2)
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Now, we'll differentiate \( F'(x) \) to find the second derivative.

Again, use the **product rule** on each term of \( F'(x) \).

Let:
- \( u = e^x (2x + x^2) \)

But for clarity, let's consider it as two separate terms multiplied by \( e^x \):
\[
F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

Now, differentiate each term separately.

1. **First Term: \( 2x e^x \)**
   - Let \( u = 2x \), \( v = e^x \)
   - \( u' = 2 \), \( v' = e^x \)
   
   Applying the product rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (2x e^x) = 2 e^x + 2x e^x
   \]

2. **Second Term: \( x^2 e^x \)**
   - Let \( u = x^2 \), \( v = e^x \)
   - \( u' = 2x \), \( v' = e^x \)
   
   Applying the product rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (x^2 e^x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]

### Step 3: Combine the Results
Add the derivatives of both terms to get \( F''(x) \):
\[
F''(x) = (2 e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x)
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
F''(x) = 2 e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

Factor out \( e^x \):
\[
F''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is:

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{e^{x} \left(x^{2} + 4 x + 2\right)}

The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) can be found using the chain rule and the power rule.

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Let \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \).
2. Then, \( F(x) = \frac{1}{g(x)} = g(x)^{-1} \).
3. Find the derivative of \( g(x) \): \( g'(x) = 2x \).
4. Apply the chain rule: \( F'(x) = -1 \cdot g(x)^{-2} \cdot g'(x) = -\frac{g'(x)}{(g(x))^2} \).
5. Substitute \( g(x) \) and \( g'(x) \): 
   \( F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \).

Final Answer: \boxed{-\dfrac{2 x}{\left(x^{2}+1\right)^{2}}}

Let me try another value, say x=2. F'(2) should be 2/2 = 1. Using the limit definition: [F(2+h) - F(2)] / h ≈ [ln((2+h)^2) - ln(4)] / h = [2 ln(2+h) - 2 ln(2)] / h = 2[ln(2+h) - ln(2)] / h.

The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \) is calculated by first finding the first derivative using the product rule, then applying the product rule again for the second derivative.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x))**:
   - Let \( u = x^3 \) and \( v = \ln x \).
   - Compute derivatives: \( u' = 3x^2 \) and \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \).
   - Apply product rule: \( F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \).

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x))**:
   - Differentiate each term of \( F'(x) \):
     - For \( 3x^2 \ln x \), apply product rule again with \( u = 3x^2 \) and \( v = \ln x \).
       - \( u' = 6x \) and \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \).
       - Result: \( 6x \ln x + 3x \).

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if a function is the product of two functions, its derivative can be found by taking the derivative of the first function times the second function plus the first function times the derivative of the second function.

Let's define:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

Simplify each term:
\[
1 \cdot \ln x = \ln x
\]
\[
x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 1
\]

Add the simplified terms together to get the final derivative:
\[
F'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \) is:

\boxed{\ln x + 1}

The function we are differentiating is \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\). Using the quotient rule, which states that if \(f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)}\), then \(f'(x) = \frac{v(x)u'(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}\).

Let:
- \(u(x) = \sin x\) ⇒ \(u'(x) = \cos x\)
- \(v(x) = x^2 + 1\) ⇒ \(v'(x) = 2x\)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1)(\cos x) - (\sin x)(2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

Simplifying, we get:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

Final Answer: \(\boxed{\dfrac{(x^{2}+1)\cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}\)

The problem involves finding the second derivative of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \).

1. **First Derivative**: Using the chain rule, we find:
   \[
   f'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]

2. **Second Derivative**: Applying the product rule to \( 2x \cdot e^{x^2} \):
   \[
   f''(x) = (2x)' \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (e^{x^2})' 
   \]
   Calculate each part:
   - Derivative of \( 2x \): \( 2 \)
   - Derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) using chain rule: \( 2x \cdot e^{x^2} \)

   Plugging these in:
   \[
   f''(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (2x \cdot e^{x^2}) 
   \]
   Simplify the terms:
   \[
   f''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
   \]

3. **Combine Like Terms**:
   Factor out \( 2e^{x^2} \):
   \[
   f''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)
   \]
   Which simplifies to:
   \[
   f''(x) = (2 + 4x^2)e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The second derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) is \( (2 + 4x^2)e^{x^2} \).

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the given expression is found by examining the leading coefficients of the highest-degree terms in the numerator and denominator.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the degrees of the polynomials:**
   - Numerator degree: 2 (from \(3x^2\))
   - Denominator degree: 2 (from \(5x^2\))

2. **Since both have equal degrees, compare leading coefficients:**
   - Leading coefficient in numerator: 3
   - Leading coefficient in denominator: 5

The limit evaluates to 1/2 using either Taylor series expansion or L'Hospital's Rule.

Step-by-Step Explanation:

We want to find:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2}
\]

**Method 1: Using Taylor Series Expansion**

1. **Expand cos(x) around x = 0:**
   The Taylor series for cos(x) is:
   \[
   \cos x = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \cdots
   \]
   
2. **Substitute the expansion into the numerator:**
   For small \(x\), higher-order terms (like \(x^4\)) are negligible, so:
   \[
   1 - \cos x \approx 1 - \left(1 - \frac{x^2}{2}\right) = \frac{x^2}{2}
   \]

3. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} \approx \frac{\frac{x^2}{2}}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

**Method 2: Using L'Hospital's Rule**

1. **Identify the indeterminate form:**
   As \(x \to 0\), both numerator and denominator approach 0, so we can apply L'Hospital's Rule.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule once:**
   Differentiate numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{-\sin x}{2x}
   \]
   
3. **Still an indeterminate form, apply L'Hospital's Rule again:**
   Differentiate numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\cos x}{2} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\text{Final Answer: } \boxed{\frac{1}{2}}
\]

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right)
\]

Multiply numerator and denominator by the conjugate:

\[
= \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

Simplify numerator:

\[
= \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{(x^2 + 2x) - x^2}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]
\[
= \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

Divide numerator and denominator by \( x \):

\[
= \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
\]
As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \frac{2}{x} \) approaches 0:

\[
= \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1
\]

The problem requires finding the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\sin(2x)}{x} \).

1. **Substitution**: Directly substituting \( x = 0 \) results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \), so we need another method.

2. **L'Hospital's Rule**: Applying L'Hospital's Rule:
   - Differentiate the numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}[\sin(2x)] = 2\cos(2x) \)
   - Differentiate the denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1 \)
   - The limit becomes \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{2\cos(2x)}{1} = 2 \cdot \cos(0) = 2 \).

3. **Standard Limit**: Using the known result \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(kx)}{x} = k \), we get:
   - Here, \( k = 2 \), so the limit is \( 2 \).

4. **Series Expansion**: Expanding \( \sin(2x) \) as a Taylor series around 0 gives approximately \( 2x \). Thus,
   - \( \frac{\sin(2x)}{x} \approx \frac{2x}{x} = 2 \).

5. **Substitution Method**: Let \( t = 2x \), then:
   - As \( x \to 0 \), \( t \to 0 \).
   - The limit becomes \( \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{\sin(t)}{t/2} = 2 \cdot \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{\sin(t)}{t} = 2 \).

In all methods, the limit evaluates to 2.

**Final Answer:**
The value of the limit is 2.

To solve the given limit correctly, let's first clarify the expression. The function appears to be:

\[
\frac{x^3 + 1}{x^3 - x^2 + 1}
\]

As there is no specified point for \( x \) in the problem statement, I'll assume we are finding the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Expression Analysis:**
   The function simplifies to:
   \[
   f(x) = \frac{x^3 + 1}{x^3 - x^2 + 1}
   \]

2. **Identify the Behavior as \( x \to \infty \):**
   When \( x \) approaches infinity, we focus on the highest degree terms in both numerator and denominator.

3. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by the Highest Power of \( x \):**
   In this case, divide each term by \( x^3 \):
   \[
   f(x) = \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x^3}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^3}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   As \( x \to \infty \), terms with \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^3} \) approach 0.
   
5. **Simplify:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} f(x) = \frac{1 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is \( 1 \).

But just to be thorough, maybe I should use L'Hospital's Rule here since both the numerator and denominator are approaching infinity as x approaches infinity. L'Hospital's Rule says that if you have an indeterminate form like ∞/∞ or 0/0, you can take the derivative of the numerator and the derivative of the denominator separately and then take the limit again.

So applying L'Hospital's Rule: The derivative of ln x is 1/x, and the derivative of x is 1. So now we have (1/x)/1, which simplifies to 1/x.

Now taking the limit as x approaches infinity of 1/x. As x gets larger and larger without bound, 1/x gets closer and closer to zero. Therefore, the limit should be zero.

Wait, just to double-check, if I plug in a really large number for x, like a million or a billion, ln x is definitely not going to reach anywhere near that big of a number as x itself. So yeah, it makes sense that (ln x)/x would approach zero.
</think>

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{\ln x}{x} \) is determined by analyzing the growth rates of the numerator and denominator. Since \( \ln x \) grows much slower than \( x \), the ratio approaches 0.

- The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is \( 6x \).
- The derivative of \( -5x \) is \( -5 \).
- The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \) is \( 0 \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules.

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

Okay, so I need to solve this limit problem where it's asking for the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \). Hmm, derivatives can sometimes be tricky, but let me think step by step. 

First, I remember that the derivative measures how a function changes as its input changes. In calculus, we use the concept of limits to find derivatives because they involve approaching a point infinitesimally close.

The function given is \( \frac{1}{x} \), which can also be written as \( x^{-1} \). I recall that there's a general rule for finding derivatives of functions like this. Specifically, the power rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = x^n \), its derivative \( f'(x) \) is \( n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Applying this to \( x^{-1} \), let's set \( n = -1 \). So according to the power rule, the derivative should be \( -1 \cdot x^{-2} \), right? That simplifies to \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

Wait a second, did I do that correctly? Let me double-check. If I have \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n x^{n-1} \). Plugging in \( n = -1 \), we get \( f'(x) = (-1)x^{-2} \), which is indeed \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

Is there another way to verify this? Maybe using the limit definition of a derivative. The derivative can also be expressed as:

\[ f'(a) = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{f(a + h) - f(a)}{h} \]

Let's apply this to \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \). So,

\[ f'(a) = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{\frac{1}{a + h} - \frac{1}{a}}{h} \]

To simplify the numerator, I'll find a common denominator:

\[ \frac{1}{a + h} - \frac{1}{a} = \frac{a - (a + h)}{a(a + h)} = \frac{-h}{a(a + h)} \]

Now substitute this back into the limit:

\[ f'(a) = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{\frac{-h}{a(a + h)}}{h} = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{-h}{a(a + h)h} \]

The \( h \) in the numerator and denominator cancels out:

\[ f'(a) = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{-1}{a(a + h)} \]

As \( h \) approaches 0, \( a + h \) becomes just \( a \), so:

\[ f'(a) = \frac{-1}{a^2} \]

That's the same result as before. So using both the power rule and the limit definition confirms that the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) is \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

I think I got it right. The key steps were recognizing the function could be rewritten with a negative exponent, applying the power rule correctly, and then verifying using the limit process to ensure there were no mistakes.

f'(x) = sin(x)*0 + cos(x)*1
= cos(x)

Step-by-step explanation:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = e^x
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = (1) \cdot e^x + x \cdot (e^x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
\]

The derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(x^2) \) is calculated by applying logarithmic properties and differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Simplify the Function:**
   - Use the property \( \ln(a^b) = b\ln(a) \).
   - So, \( F(x) = 2\ln(x) \).

2. **Differentiate Using Known Rule:**
   - The derivative of \( \ln(x) \) with respect to x is \( \frac{1}{x} \).
   - Applying the constant multiple rule, multiply by 2.

3. **Final Derivative:**
   - \( F'(x) = 2 \times \frac{1}{x} = \frac{2}{x} \).

The derivative of \( e^{\ln(\sin x)} \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using the chain rule. 

1. Let \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \).
2. The function we're differentiating is \( e^{f(x)} \).
3. Using the chain rule: \( \frac{d}{dx} e^{f(x)} = e^{f(x)} \cdot f'(x) \).
4. Compute \( f'(x) \): 
   - \( f'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \cot x \).
5. Therefore, the derivative is:
   - \( e^{\ln(\sin x)} \cdot \cot x = \sin x \cdot \cot x = \cos x \).

The derivative of the function \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) with respect to \(x\) is found using the quotient rule. 

First, identify:
- Numerator: \(u = \sin x\), so \(u' = \cos x\)
- Denominator: \(v = x^2 + 1\), so \(v' = 2x\)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \]

Simplifying, we get:
\[\boxed{\frac{\cos x(x^2 + 1) - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}\]

To find the second derivative of EP(x) = e^{x^2}, we proceed as follows:

   f'(x) = d/dx [e^{x²}] = 2x * e^{x²}

First, I need to evaluate the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of \( \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1} \).

I notice that both the numerator and the denominator approach zero when \( x = 1 \), resulting in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \). To resolve this, I can factor the numerator.

The expression \( x^3 - 1 \) is a difference of cubes, which can be factored using the formula \( a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2) \).

Applying this formula, we get:
\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

Now, substitute the factored form back into the original expression:
\[
\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1} = \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
\]

Since \( x \neq 1 \), we can cancel out the \( (x - 1) \) terms:
\[
\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1
\]

Finally, evaluate the simplified expression as \( x \) approaches 1:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
\]
</think>

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   We need to evaluate the limit of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1} \) as \( x \) approaches 1.

2. **Check Direct Substitution:**
   If we directly substitute \( x = 1 \) into the function:
   \[
   f(1) = \frac{1^3 - 1}{1 - 1} = \frac{0}{0}
   \]
   This results in an indeterminate form. Therefore, direct substitution doesn't work, and we need to manipulate the expression.

3. **Factor the Numerator:**
   Notice that \( x^3 - 1 \) is a difference of cubes. We can factor it using the formula:
   \[
   a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2)
   \]
   Here, \( a = x \) and \( b = 1 \), so:
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Substitute the factored form back into the original function:
   \[
   f(x) = \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
   \]
   Since \( x \neq 1 \), we can cancel out the \( (x - 1) \) terms:
   \[
   f(x) = x^2 + x + 1
   \]

5. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   Now, substitute \( x = 1 \) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 1}} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

6. **Final Answer:**
   The limit of \( f(x) \) as \( x \) approaches 1 is:
   \[
   \boxed{3}
   \]

The limit of sqrt(x^2 + 2x) minus x as x approaches infinity can be evaluated by several methods:

1. **Rationalization**: Multiply numerator and denominator by the conjugate.
   - The expression becomes (sqrt(x²+2x)-x)(sqrt(x²+2x)+x)/(sqrt(x²+2x)+x) = (x² + 2x - x²)/[sqrt(x²+2x)+x] = (2x)/(sqrt(x²+2x)+x).
   - Dividing numerator and denominator by x gives [2]/(sqrt(1 + 2/x) + 1). As x approaches infinity, this simplifies to 2/(1 +1)=1.

2. **Substitution**: Let t = 1/x, then the limit becomes lim_{t->0+} (sqrt(1+2t)-1)/t * (1/t).
   - Using L’Hospital’s Rule: lim_{t->0} [sqrt(1+2t) -1]/t = derivative of sqrt(1 + 2t) at t=0, which is 1. Thus the limit is 1*1=1.

3. **Taylor Series Expansion**: Expand sqrt(1 + 2/x) around x=infinity.
   - The expansion gives sqrt(1+2/x)=1 + (1/x) - (1)/(2x²)+..., so the expression approaches 1 as x increases.

All methods consistently yield a limit of 1.

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} \) is calculated by recognizing the Taylor series expansion of \( \tan x \) around 0, which includes terms up to \( x^3 \). Subtracting these from \( x \) cancels out the lower-degree terms, leaving only higher-order infinitesimals. Simplifying leads directly to the limit value.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Recognize the Indeterminate Form:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, both numerator (\( x - \tan x \)) and denominator (\( x^3 \)) approach 0, resulting in a 0/0 indeterminate form.

2. **Use Taylor Series Expansion of \( \tan x \):**
   - The expansion is: 
     \[
     \tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + o(x^3)
     \]
   
3. **Subtract and Simplify the Numerator:**
   - Subtracting \( \tan x \) from \( x \):
     \[
     x - \tan x = x - \left( x + \frac{x^3}{3} + o(x^3) \right) = -\frac{x^3}{3} + o(x^3)
     \]
   
4. **Divide by Denominator and Take the Limit:**
   - Dividing numerator and denominator:
     \[
     \frac{-\frac{x^3}{3}}{x^3} = -\frac{1}{3}
     \]
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, higher-order terms become negligible.

**Final Answer:**

The limit is \(- \frac{1}{3}\).

To find the limit \(\lim_{x \to 1^+} \frac{\sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 2^{1/3}}{x - 1}\), we can use several methods to evaluate it.

First, recognize that this limit is of the form \(\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}\) as \(x\) approaches \(a\), which is the definition of the derivative of \(f\) at \(a\). Here, \(a = 1\) and \(f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3}\).

To find this limit, we compute the derivative of \(f(x)\):

\[ f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \]
\[ f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (1 + x^3)^{1/3} = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}} \]

Evaluating this derivative at \(x = 1\):

\[ f'(1) = \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{(2)^{2/3}} = 2^{-2/3} \]

This can be written as:

\[ 2^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}} \]

Thus, the limit is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}
\]

     \[

The derivative of \( F(x) = \ln x \) is calculated using known differentiation rules or by applying the definition of derivatives and logarithmic properties.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Function:** The function given is \( F(x) = \ln x \), where ln denotes the natural logarithm with base e.

2. **Apply Derivative Rule:** The derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to x is a standard result in calculus, which states that:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
   \]

3. **Verification (Optional):**
    - **Definition Approach:** Using the limit definition,
      \[
      F'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\ln(x + h) - \ln x}{h}
      \]
      Simplify using logarithm properties:
      \[
      \ln\left(\frac{x + h}{x}\right) = \ln\left(1 + \frac{h}{x}\right)
      \]
      Substitute \( t = \frac{h}{x} \), so as \( h \to 0 \), \( t \to 0 \):
      \[
      F'(x) = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + t)}{x t} = \frac{1}{x} \cdot \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + t)}{t} = \frac{1}{x}
      \]
    - **Inverse Function Approach:** Let \( y = \ln x \), then \( e^y = x \). Differentiating both sides:
      \[
      e^y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{e^y} = \frac{1}{x}
      \]

4. **Conclusion:** The derivative of \( F(x) = \ln x \) is:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( F(x) = \ln x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \).

The derivative of \( y = x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found by taking the natural logarithm of both sides, differentiating implicitly, and then solving for \( dy/dx \). Using logarithmic differentiation simplifies the process.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**  
   \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) \).
3. **Simplify using logarithmic identity:**  
   \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).
4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**  
   - Left side: \( \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).
   - Right side: Apply the product rule to \( x \cdot \ln(x) \):
     \( \frac{d}{dx} [x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
5. **Set the derivatives equal:**  
   \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**  
   Multiply both sides by \( y \):
   \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1) \).
7. **Substitute back \( y = x^x \):**  
   \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1) \).

The final derivative is:

\[
\boxed{x^x (1 + \ln(x))}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \), we can use the **product rule** from calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( F'(x) \) is given by:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = e^{x} \)

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x}) = e^{x}
\]

**Step 3: Apply the product rule**

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^{x} + x \cdot e^{x}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the expression**

Factor out \( e^{x} \):

\[
F'(x) = e^{x}(1 + x)
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \) is:

\[
F'(x) = (x + 1)e^{x}
\]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{(x+1)e^{x}}

The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) can be found using the chain rule.

First, rewrite \( F(x) \) as:
\[ F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \]

Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \), so \( F(u) = u^{-1} \).

The derivative of \( F \) with respect to \( u \) is:
\[ \frac{dF}{du} = -u^{-2} \]

Next, find the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \):
\[ \frac{du}{dx} = 2x \]

Using the chain rule:
\[ \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = (-u^{-2}) \cdot (2x) \]

Substitute \( u = x^2 + 1 \) back into the equation:
\[ \frac{dF}{dx} = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we can use the chain rule for differentiation.

1. **Differentiate the outer function**: The outer function is the natural logarithm, so its derivative with respect to its argument (\( \sin x \)) is \( \frac{1}{\sin x} \).

2. **Multiply by the derivative of the inner function**: The inner function is \( \sin x \), whose derivative with respect to \( x \) is \( \cos x \).

Putting it all together:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \cot x
\]

The second derivative of F(x) = x³ ln x is found by first applying the product rule to find F'(x), then differentiating again using the product rule and power rule. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x)):**
   - Let u = x³ ⇒ u' = 3x².
   - Let v = ln x ⇒ v' = 1/x.
   - Applying the product rule: F'(x) = u'v + uv' = 3x² ln x + x³ (1/x) = 3x² ln x + x².

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x)):**
   - Differentiate each term of F'(x):
     - For the first term, d/dx [3x² ln x]:
       - Let u = 3x² ⇒ u' = 6x.
       - v remains ln x ⇒ v' = 1/x.
       - Applying product rule: (6x)(ln x) + (3x²)(1/x) = 6x ln x + 3x.
     - For the second term, d/dx [x²] = 2x.
   - Combine results: F''(x) = 6x ln x + 3x + 2x = 6x ln x + 5x.

The derivative of \( \tan^{-1} x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using implicit differentiation. Let \( y = \tan^{-1} x \), then \( \tan y = x \). Differentiating both sides gives:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[\tan y] = \frac{d}{dx}[x]
\]

Using the chain rule on the left side:

\[
\sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):

\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
\]

Since \( \tan y = x \), we can express \( \sec^2 y \) in terms of \( x \). Using a right triangle where the opposite side is \( x \) and the adjacent side is 1, the hypotenuse is \( \sqrt{x^2 + 1} \). Thus:

\[
\sec y = \frac{\sqrt{x^2 + 1}}{1} \implies \sec^2 y = x^2 + 1
\]

Therefore,

\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
= (1) \cdot (\ln x) + (x) \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
\]
\[
= \ln x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\ln x + 1}

The problem requires finding the derivative of the function \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \).

1. **Identify the components for differentiation**:
   - Outer function: \( e^u \), where \( u = x^2 \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate using the chain rule**:
   - Derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \): \( \frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u \)
   - Derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \): \( \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x \)

3. **Combine using the chain rule**:
   \[
   f'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

The final derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**  
{Final Answer: "2x e^{x²}"}

The answer is {Final Answer: "2x e^{x²}"}

Okay, so I need to find the derivative of the function F(x) = x / (1 + x²). Hmm, let's see. I remember that when you have a function like this, which is a quotient of two functions, you should use the quotient rule for differentiation. The quotient rule says that if you have a function f(x)/g(x), its derivative is [f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)] divided by [g(x)] squared.

First, I need to identify f(x) and g(x). In this case, F(x) is x divided by (1 + x²). So, f(x) should be the numerator, which is just x, and g(x) is the denominator, which is 1 plus x squared. 

Now, let's find the derivatives of f and g. The derivative of f(x) = x is straightforward; it's 1 because the slope of x with respect to x is always 1. For g(x) = 1 + x², I'll use the power rule. The derivative of x squared is 2x, so g'(x) is 2x.

Putting these into the quotient rule formula: [f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)] / [g(x)]² becomes [1*(1 + x²) - x*(2x)] divided by (1 + x²) squared.

Let me compute the numerator step by step. First, 1 times (1 + x²) is just (1 + x²). Then, x times 2x is 2x². So subtracting that from the previous term gives us (1 + x² - 2x²). Combining like terms, x squared minus 2x squared is negative x squared, so the numerator simplifies to (1 - x²).

So putting it all together, the derivative F'(x) is (1 - x²) divided by (1 + x²) squared. I think that's correct, but let me double-check my steps.

Wait, did I compute f'(x) correctly? Yes, because d/dx of x is 1. And g(x) = 1 + x², so its derivative is indeed 2x. Then plugging into the formula: numerator becomes (1*(1 + x²)) - (x*2x) which is 1 + x² - 2x² = 1 - x². The denominator is correct as well, it's squared because of the quotient rule.

Okay, so I'm confident that F'(x) = (1 - x²)/(1 + x²)^2.
</think>

The derivative of \( \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) with respect to \( x \) is found using the quotient rule. 

Let \( f(x) = x \) and \( g(x) = 1 + x^2 \). Then:

- \( f'(x) = 1 \)
- \( g'(x) = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2} = \frac{1(1 + x^2) - x(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
Simplifying the numerator:
\[
1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

Thus, \( F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} \).
